README Files

README files are a way to document the contents and structure of a folder, helping users locate the information they need. You can use a README to document changes to a repository, and explain how files are named. Please consider adding a README to each repository or including ‘README’ in the name of your overview document.

Archive Files

The following files are not ZIP archives: choice_data.csv.gz, free_sort_data.csv.gz, input_devices.csv.gz, learning_data.csv.gz, choice_data.csv.gz, dim_counts.csv.gz, first_visits.csv.gz, free_sort_data.csv.gz, input_devices.csv.gz, learning_data.csv.gz, time_totals.csv.gz, visit_sequences.csv.gz, continuous-attribute-choice-task.csv.gz, cactus-v2.csv.gz. We recommend the .zip format for archives: only ZIP stores its file listing in a way that lets a tool inspect the contents without downloading the whole archive, so a .zip is more discoverable and re-usable than a .7z, .rar, .tar or .tar.gz.

Registration’s Source Project Is Closed

1 OSF source project is closed. The files metacheck lists below were not read from it — they were retrieved from the OSF registration’s own copy, which is separate from and unaffected by the project’s access setting. Nothing is currently lost, but this is still worth fixing: a closed source project means the files only survive for as long as the registration’s copy does, and anyone who follows the project link itself (rather than the registration) will find it inaccessible.

  • https://osf.io/px2sr is closed; its files were retrieved from the OSF registration https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/7MEYK, OSF.IO/7MEYK.

Consider making the source project openly available.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 20 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 29 distinct packages/libraries: argparse, arviz, bambi, cactus_paper, dataclasses, functools, gzip, io, itertools, jax, json, matplotlib, numpy, numpyro, os, pandas, pathlib, re, rpy2, scipy, seaborn, shutil, sklearn, sys, tempfile, time, types, typing, zlib. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 33 columns across 6 data files.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 6 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

Files are missing from your deposit.

  • fig_ms_slope_readout.py reads slope_readout_profile_draws.npz.
  • fig_ms1_learning.py reads dim_counts.csv.

The script stops at the line that reads the file.

  1. Find the file on your computer.
  2. Add it to the deposit, in the folder where the script looks for it.
  3. If you cannot share it, for example for privacy reasons, say so in the README, and explain where readers can get it and where to put it.

A function or option has changed since you wrote the code, so it stops with today’s version of the package.

  1. In R, run renv::init() in your project and add the renv.lock file this creates to the deposit; in Python, run pip freeze > requirements.txt and add that file.
  2. State your version of R or Python in the README.
  3. Better still, update the code so it also works with current versions.

A package could not be installed.

  • src/cactus_paper/analyses/prereg.py needs rpy2.

rpy2 could not be installed with the current version of R, probably because it is old. Record the versions you used (in R, renv::init() writes them to a renv.lock file) and your R version. To be sure the code keeps running, provide a Docker image: a frozen copy of the whole computer setup the code needs, which you can build from rocker/r-ver at your R version.

A script waits for a person to choose a file, answer a question, or give arguments on the command line.

  • contribution.py

Nobody can answer when the code is checked automatically. Write the file name into the script, and put the exact command with its arguments in the README.

Execution

The Python code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 26 scripts: 1 ran without error, 20 errored, 0 timed out, 1 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse, and 0 are written for Python 2 and not run. 3 files were not run on their own, because other files import them: src/cactus_paper/init.py (by fig2_task.py, fig3_poles.py, fig_ms1_learning.py, fig_ms4_model.py, fig_ms5_crossstream.py, fig_ms_model.py, fig_ms_readout.py, fig_ms_slope_readout.py, fig_ms_valuemap.py, fig_supp_knowledge.py, antecedents.py, contribution.py, crossstream.py, src/cactus_paper/analyses/prereg.py, robustness.py, value_map.py, build.py, choice.py, context.py, fits.py, knowledge.py, learning.py, model.py, src/cactus_paper/manuscript/prereg.py, search.py, validation.py); src/cactus_paper/analyses/init.py (by fig_ms5_crossstream.py, fig_ms_readout.py, fig_ms_slope_readout.py); constants.py (by fig3_poles.py, fig_ms1_learning.py, fig_ms_valuemap.py, antecedents.py, src/cactus_paper/analyses/prereg.py, robustness.py, value_map.py, context.py, fits.py, knowledge.py, learning.py, model.py, search.py); src/cactus_paper/data/init.py (by src/cactus_paper/analyses/prereg.py, robustness.py, value_map.py); device.py (by robustness.py); sample.py (by src/cactus_paper/analyses/prereg.py, robustness.py, value_map.py); cards.py (by fig2_task.py, fig_ms5_crossstream.py); plotting.py (by fig3_poles.py, fig_ms1_learning.py, fig_ms4_model.py, fig_ms5_crossstream.py, fig_ms_model.py, fig_ms_readout.py, fig_ms_slope_readout.py, fig_ms_valuemap.py, fig_supp_knowledge.py). Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions (e.g. in a requirements.txt). 1 script stopped on a file another script writes, so it was run once more after the other scripts: fig_ms1_learning.py (choice_data.csv, learning_data.csv). 2 files were written in another folder than where the script looked, so before that run we copied them there: data/processed/exp2/choice_data.csv to data/choice_data.csv.gz/choice_data.csv for fig_ms1_learning.py; data/processed/exp2/learning_data.csv to data/learning_data.csv.gz/learning_data.csv for fig_ms1_learning.py. The scripts assume they all run from one folder; saying which, or building paths from the top of the project (as with here::here()), solves this. Which script must run first can be stated in a README, or shown by numbering the file names (01_, 02_). A script looked for 3 files in another folder than the deposit has them, so before running it once more we copied them there: learning_data.csv.gz/learning_data.csv to data/processed/exp2/learning_data.csv for fig_ms1_learning.py; choice_data.csv.gz/choice_data.csv to data/processed/exp2/choice_data.csv for fig_ms1_learning.py; first_visits.csv.gz/first_visits.csv to data/processed/exp2/first_visits.csv for fig_ms1_learning.py. Building paths from the top of the project (as with here::here()), or keeping the folders the code expects, solves this.

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

Participants: We recruited 159 participants using the Prolific research platform.

Participants were paid $8 an hour for their participation as well as a flat $3 bonus for completing the task.

Participants completed learning blocks for each dimension sequentially (order randomized).

Multi-Dimensional Decision Task: After learning, participants completed a multi-dimensional decision task in which they were asked to choose between two multi-attribute options presented as columns.

Participants completed 120 trials, with a self-paced break (up to one minute) at the midpoint.

No feedback was provided on any trial, though participants were informed that their choices accrued points toward their bonus.

Before the main task, participants completed six practice trials with the mouse-contingent display in which they searched an occluded display for a single target stimulus (“Which side has the purple?”), familiarizing them with the aperture and the response buttons.

Participants were given 3000ms to judge which item was higher or lower according to a prompt.

Furthermore, participants differed widely in these weights, such that participants were not all weighting the same dimension highly and the other two lower (M = +1.98, 95% HDI [+1.76, +2.18]).

If participants were using a pure directional strategy, it should not matter whether the left side wins by +25 or by +100 (Figure 4 C purple line).

If instead participants were using each value difference, one would expect an equivalent change in choice for each of the Δvalue increments (Figure 4 C orange line).

We found that participants had a wide variance in how they sampled the multi-dimensional decision task stimulus display while comparing the two options (mean -0.20, SD 0.30, range -0.80 to +0.74; 21% above 0; Figure 5 A for examples).

In literature on cue combination, participants are shown to weight dimensions according to reliability, such that dimensions that are more reliable are given higher weight in the composite stimulus (Ernst and Banks 2002; Bankieris, Bejjanki, and Aslin 2017).

All participants gave informed consent, and the study was approved by the University of Chicago Institutional Review Board (IRB20-1324).

Ethics approval statement:

The experiment was approved by the University of Chicago SSD IRB (IRB number: IRB20-1324).

All participants gave informed consent, and the study was approved by the University of Chicago Institutional Review Board (IRB20-1324).

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

id title template
7meyk Inferring Categorical vs Relational Value Construction from Attribute-Sampling Behavior OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

id sample_size
7meyk We plan to collect N = 80 participants after applying exclusion criteria.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Inferring Categorical vs Relational Value Construction from Attribute-Sampling Behavior
id 7meyk
link https://osf.io/7meyk
date_created 2026-02-02T20:23:06.267548
date_modified 2026-02-02T21:00:03.525411
date_registered 2026-02-02T20:23:06.230245
embargo_end_date
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-7meyk-v1
research_questions H1: Sampling Pattern Reflect Computational Demands
Hypothesis: The two valuation policies impose distinct attentional demands. The relational policy (constructing value) is fundamentally comparative, requiring the decision-maker to toggle between options to detect local differences (akin to flipping back and forth between two similar kitchens). In contrast, the categorical policy (retrieving value) treats options as independent entities, requiring only that features be identified and their values retrieved from memory.
Prediction: On divergent trials, the relative allocation of sampling to within-dimension comparisons (vs. between-dimension transitions) will predict choice. Greater within-dimension sampling will be associated with higher probability of choosing the relational winner; greater between-dimension sampling will be associated with higher probability of choosing the categorical winner.
H2: Discriminability Shifts Sampling Strategy
Hypothesis: The efficiency of the categorical policy depends on the ability to map a visual feature to a specific value category. When perceptual discriminability is low (e.g., the features are highly similar but on opposite ends of a boundary between categories), the uncertainty of retrieval increases. To resolve this uncertainty, the decision-maker must abandon the efficient categorical strategy and engage the relational policy to resolve the difference via direct visual comparison.
Prediction: As within-dimension discriminability decreases (i.e., the two stimuli on a dimension become more perceptually similar), participants increase within-dimension sampling on that specific dimension.
H3: Individual Differences in Category Knowledge Predict Policy Reliance
Hypothesis (Main Effect): The stability of the categorical policy depends on the strength of the underlying memory representations. Individuals with robust category knowledge (stronger priors) can retrieve values more reliably and with less cognitive effort than those with weaker knowledge, leading to a general bias toward the memory-guided categorical policy.
Prediction: Participants with higher learning accuracy (category knowledge) will select the categorical winner significantly more often on divergent trials compared to participants with lower accuracy.
H3b: Knowledge Moderates the Impact of Uncertainty
Hypothesis: Strong category knowledge buffers against perceptual uncertainty. Just as an expert realtor can instantly categorize a kitchen that looks ambiguous to a novice, participants with high category knowledge should be able to maintain a categorical policy even when discriminability is reduced. In contrast, participants with weak knowledge will be forced to switch to the relational policy (comparison) more rapidly as discriminability drops.
Prediction: We predict a Knowledge × Discriminability interaction on sampling behavior. The tendency to increase relational sampling (within-dimension transitions) in response to low discriminability will be attenuated for participants with high category knowledge.
foreknowledge_of_data_or_evidence Data does not yet exist. No part of the data that will be used for this analysis plan exists, and no part will be generated until after this plan is registered.
study_type Randomized Experiment: Must include random assignment of subjects to treatments or conditions. This usually includes lab experiments, field experiments, intervention experiments, randomized controlled trials, and A/B testing.
intention_for_causal_interpretation No causal relationship inferred: This study is not intended to inform a causal relationship.
blinding No blinding is involved.
study_design_overview Learning Task: Participants learn value-category mappings for three perceptually uniform dimensions:
1. Shape: Radial frequency patterns drawn from a validated circular shape space (Li, Liang, Lee, & Barense, 2020, J Exp Psychol Gen)
2. Color: Hues drawn from CIELAB perceptually uniform color space
3. Angle: Orientation of an indicator line within a circle, ranging from 0–230°
Each dimension is divided into five value categories (e.g., for a given dimension: Category 1 = 0 points, Category 2 = 25 points, Category 3 = 50 points, Category 4 = 75, Category 5 100 points). The mapping direction (ascending vs. descending along the perceptual continuum) is counterbalanced across participants.
On each learning trial, participants see two stimuli from different value categories on the same dimension and select the higher- or lower-value option (prompt alternates every 10 trials). Feedback shows correctness and reveals both values. Participants complete learning blocks for each dimension sequentially (order randomized).
Free-Sorting Task: After learning each dimension, participants perform a two-stage free-sort with 16 items spanning that dimension: first sorting by perceptual similarity, then re-sorting by value.
Novel Choice Task: Participants complete 120 trials. On each trial, two multi-dimensional options are presented as columns, each containing one stimulus from each dimension (row positions randomized but matched across columns). Participants select the option with higher aggregate value. No feedback is provided.
Our main trials of interest are those where the categorical policy and relational policy point to separate options. We label these trials divergent trials. These arise when one dimension exhibits a large value difference that outweighs two smaller opposing differences on the remaining dimensions. That single dimension is called the critical dimension. For example, if Option A scores [80, 20, 20] and Option B scores [20, 40, 40] across three dimensions, then: - Categorical winner = Option A (summed EV: 120 vs. 100) - Relational winner = Option B (wins on 2 of 3 dimensions)
As there are 3 dimensions, we will generate an equivalent number of divergent trials where the critical dimension is shape, color, and line orientation (20 trials each). Overall, there will be 60 divergent trials. There will also be an equivalent number of non-divergent trials (where both policies point the participant towards the same option), which will serve as control trials.
Mouse-Contingent Display: Each trial begins with a 500ms full preview, after which stimuli become occluded (grey, low-resolution). Participants reveal stimuli by hovering the mouse cursor (100px aperture radius) over each location. This creates a serial processing bottleneck, allowing us to record information sampling trajectories.
randomization • Dimension-to-feature mapping: randomized across participants
• Value mapping direction: counterbalanced across participants
• Dimension learning order: randomized across participants
• Trial-level stimulus positions: randomized within constraints to achieve target divergent trial rate
data_collection_procedures Data collection: Data collection will be performed using the Prolific research platform with a jsPsych experiment posted on cognition.run.
Pre-selection criteria: Participants will be 18–36 years old, fluent English speakers, residing in the USA, and report no problems with color vision. To ensure high quality, we will also select participants who have completed 20 or more decision-making tasks on Prolific with an average acceptance rate of 100%
Exclusion criteria (applied sequentially):
1. Incomplete Data: Participants who do not complete all blocks of the experiment.
2. Learning Failure: Participants who achieve less than 60% accuracy in the final block of the learning phase for any of the three dimensions.
3. Non-Compliance: Participants who fail to uncover any information (0 mouse-overs on stimuli) on more than 5% of novel choice trials.
sample_size We plan to collect N = 80 participants after applying exclusion criteria.
sample_size_rationale We use a sensitivity power analysis (using the SIMR package) approach given uncertainty in true effect sizes from pilot data. Power analysis accounting for within-subject clustering from the pilot sample (subject standard deviation = 0.523) indicates we can detect >95% power to detect our conservative target effect (β=0.139, OR=1.15) at α=.05 if the intercept is between 0 and 2.1. These come from beta estimates run on all trials as divergent trial count was low in the pilot. Given this, a more conservative estimate would allow for N = 80 participants and approximately 60 divergent trials per participant (50% of overall trials in the novel-choice task). This represents an effect-size that is plausible given our pilot data, where we observed qualitative differences in sampling patterns across trial types despite limited divergent trial counts.
Pilot data (N = 27, ~18% divergent trials) showed directional effects consistent with H1 and H2, though confidence intervals were wide due to sparse divergent trials. The current design increases divergent trial frequency to improve precision.
stopping_rule We plan to collect data as soon as this pre-registration is posted. Participant data will be run through an automated pipeline that only applies exclusion criteria. Participants will be collected on prolific until we have reached our designated sample size.
manipulated_variables - Dimension-to-feature mapping: randomized across participants
- Value mapping direction: counterbalanced across participants
- Dimension learning order: randomized across participants
- Trial-level stimulus positions: randomized within constraints to achieve target divergent trial rate
- Composition and temporal placement of divergent trials
measured_variables - choices and response times in learning task
- mouse movements and final positions in the free-sorting task
- choices, response times, and mouse movements/positions in the novel choice task
indices All sampling metrics are derived from mouse trajectory data recorded via the gaze-aperture display.
- Sampling events occur whenever a participant uncovers part of the stimulus. As the aperture is rather wide any time the center of the aperture enters a 100px radius centered on the object is deemed a sample.

- Within-Dimension Transitions: Count of consecutive sampling events where the participant moves from one stimulus to the other stimulus within the same dimension (i.e., comparing the two options on a single feature).
- Between-Dimension Transitions: Count of consecutive sampling events where the participant moves from a stimulus in one dimension to a stimulus in a different dimension (i.e., traversing down or across the option columns).

- Total Transitions: Sum of within-dimension and between-dimension transitions on a given trial.

- Within-Dimension Proportion: Ratio of within-dimension transitions to total transitions. Higher values indicate more relational-style sampling; lower values indicate more categorical-style sampling.

Note: Because total transitions = within-dimension + between-dimension transitions, these measures are linearly dependent. Analyses condition on total transitions or use the proportion directly.

Operationalizing category knowledge
This is a participant-level measure of how well the participant learned the underlying category-to-value mapping during the learning phase.
Primary operationalization: mean accuracy on the final block of the learning task, averaged across all three dimensions.

Operationalizing perceptual discriminability
The perceptual distance between the two stimuli on a given dimension. For each dimension, discriminability is computed as the absolute angular difference along the respective perceptual continuum. Lower discriminability means the two stimuli are more perceptually similar and harder to distinguish.
statistical_tests Model 1: Sampling Predicts Choice (H1)
Mixed-effects logistic regression on divergent trials:
“choice relational”∼“within dimension count”+“between dimension count”+“discriminability critical”+(1+“within dimension count” |“subject” )
- choice_relational: 1 if participant chose relational winner, 0 if categorical winner
- within dimension count: count of within-dimension transitions on that trial
- between dimension count: count of between-dimension transitions on that trial
- discriminability critical: discriminability on the critical dimension (covariate)
- Random intercepts and slopes for within dimension count by subject

Model 2: Discriminability Shifts Sampling (H2)
Mixed-effects negative binomial regression on divergent trials:
“within dimension count critical”∼“between dimension count”+“discriminability critical”+(1+“discriminability critical” |“subject” )
- within dimension count critical: count of within-dimension transitions on the critical dimension
- discriminability critical: discriminability on the critical dimension
- between dimension count: count of between-dimension transitions across all dimensions
- Random intercepts and slopes for discriminability_critical by subject

Mixed-effects logistic regression on divergent trials:
“choice categorical”∼“category knowledge”+“discriminability critical”+(1|“subject” )
- choice categorical: 1 if participant chose categorical winner, 0 otherwise
- discriminability critical: discriminability on the critical dimension
- category knowledge: participant-level learning accuracy (between-subjects predictor)
- Random intercepts by subject

Model 3b: Knowledge Moderates Discriminability Effect (H3b)
Mixed-effects negative binomial regression on divergent trials:
“within dimension count critical”∼“total transitions”+“discriminability critical × category knowledge”+(1+“discriminability critical” |“subject” )
transformations As response time is not normally distributed it will be log-transformed before being used in exploratory analyses.
inference_criteria H1 Inference: H1 is supported if the coefficient for within dimension count is positive and statistically significant (p < .05)

H2 Inference: H2 is supported if the coefficient for discriminability critical is negative and statistically significant (p < .05).

H3a Inference: H3a is supported if the coefficient for category knowledge is positive and statistically significant (p < .05).

H3b Inference: H3b is supported if the interaction coefficient is positive (i.e., the negative discriminability→sampling relationship is attenuated for high-knowledge participants).
data_exclusion_criteria Data Preprocessing
1. Compute value sums for each option on each trial; classify trials as divergent or non-divergent
2. For divergent trials, identify the categorical winner, relational winner, and critical dimension
3. Extract sampling metrics from mouse trajectory data: within-dimension transitions, between-dimension transitions, total transitions
4. Compute discriminability for each dimension on each trial
5. Compute participant-level category knowledge scores

Exclusion criteria (applied sequentially):
1. Incomplete Data: Participants who do not complete all blocks of the experiment.
2. Learning Failure: Participants who achieve less than 60% accuracy in the final block of the learning phase for any of the three dimensions.
3. Non-Compliance: Participants who fail to uncover any information (0 mouse-overs on stimuli) on more than 5% of novel choice trials.
missing_data_handling As long as participants meet our inclusion criteria we will use all available data from them and ignore missing data.
exploratory_analyses 1. Divergent vs. non-divergent comparison: Test whether sampling patterns (within-dimension proportion) differ systematically between divergent and non-divergent trials.
2. Free-sort alignment: Examine whether Adjusted Rand Index from the value-based free-sort predicts choice policy and/or sampling, as an alternative operationalization of category knowledge.
3. Endpoint effects: Verify that discriminability effects are not driven by stimuli near the perceptual dimension endpoints (0° or 230°).
4. Response time: Examine whether RT on divergent trials relates to sampling pattern and/or choice, potentially indicating strategy conflict.
paper_id input

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

When making choices between multi-dimensional options, people draw upon knowledge of the options' various properties to make a decision. While much work has probed how individuals make decisions in these modes, less is known of how learned value maps can be flexibly composed to support novel decisions. Here we taught participants three separate perceptual-to-value mappings, each dividing a continuous perceptual dimension into five value categories. We then had them choose between composite options never encountered during learning while a mouse-contingent display forced serial information sampling. How the learned map was read during choice varied widely across people, from fully metric reads (e.g. how much better) to purely ordinal ones (e.g. which is ahead). We find that ordinal comparison is not simply a degraded version of the same metric. Instead, participants mix between ordinal and metric reads. How well each dimension was learned set its weight, while how strongly learning accuracy depended on the separation between categories lowered both the weights and the metric share of the read. Both the readout and weights left separable signatures in information search. Learned value maps are thus composed and then read out to guide choice.

Introduction

We often choose between options composed of several dimensions, relying on our knowledge of each dimension to make a decision about a new composite. Consider two cocktails on a list, each described by its sweetness, sourness, and strength. What a given level of sweetness or strength is worth to you is knowledge that you acquired one drink at a time. As a result of uneven learning, you may only have a loose sense of strength but a better sense for sweetness, leading to different weighting of those dimensions when you choose a drink from the menu. Upon encountering a new drink you can work out how much you think you will enjoy it by combining your knowledge of the different dimensions.

Previous work has uncovered a set of common heuristics individuals tend to follow to make choices in these types of situations (see Gigerenzer and Gaissmaier 2011; Busemeyer et al. 2019 for review). These heuristics act as a general rules to combine dimensional values together to drive choice, or to flexibly halt deliberation when a threshold is reached. Often, the specific values are either supplied by scalar values, measured by participant ratings, or estimated from preferences. Recent work has further shown that these heuristics can be recovered from resource rational modeling where people are given differing knowledge about environmental features (Binz et al. 2022; Krueger et al. 2024). Thus, the decision strategy emerges as a function of the environmental or instructional differences, but it still unclear how individuals may differ in how they learn about and represent the same environment.

The representation of a multi-dimensional decision space may rely on the cognitive mapping system (Gärdenfors 2004; Bellmund et al. 2018; Constantinescu, O'Reilly, and Behrens 2016). In a decision space value would be graded and interpolable. Further, maps would support valuation of combinations that were never explicitly experienced (Orloff et al. 2026; Park, Miller, and Boorman 2021; Theves et al. 2021). Maps also can be warped and uneven, moving beyond the simple conception of imprecision in the representation as adding noise to valuation (G. Son, Walther, and Mack 2024; Boccara et al. 2019). Though the contents of cognitive maps have been well studied, how these maps are read at the moment of choice are less clear (Bottini et al., n.d.). In a multidimensional decision, each option can be thought of as a point in the space that a participant has not previously visited. The value of an option can then be constructed by navigating along the dimensions to reach that point.

As people gain experience with the dimensions used in their decisions, they will necessarily have varying levels of imprecision in their internal representations. This imprecision might simply add noise to the construction of an overall value estimate, or it might change how comparisons are made between options. The same map can be read for the actual size of the differences along dimensions or merely used for a coarse read of direction. For instance, if drink A is far better on sweetness and slightly worse on both sourness and strength than drink B, you might choose to go off of the overall number of attributes that drink B wins on (2/3). If you instead take the drinks on balance, you might realize that A is a better drink overall. Two people who like the same things and know them equally well can differ in these strategies; one asks how much better the winner is, the other only which drink wins in more of the flavor dimensions.

Here, we investigate how representations learned to varying precision guide value construction in multi-attribute decisions. Participants first learned three separate perceptual-to-value mappings, each dividing a continuous perceptual dimension into five value categories. They then chose between composite options assembled from all three while we recorded how they sampled the choice display. We also examined mouse-tracking data to provide further insight into how information search coupled with learned representations following from a line of process-tracing work in decision-making (Gluth, Deakin, and Rieskamp 2026; Payne 1976; Yang and Krajbich 2023). We find that how well participants learned each dimension predicts how they weight it when encountering new composites. How strongly their learning accuracy depended on the separation between categories predicted how they read their map at choice, more by direction than by distance, and also lowered their weights. Aside from choice, we find that the weighting and reading of the map leave separate traces in search processes. Overall, this work disentangles what constituent components go into value construction in novel choice. Differences in value construction may stem from variations in value maps and how they are combined and read from.

Methods

Participants: We recruited 159 participants using the Prolific research platform. Of these, 30 were excluded for insufficient engagement with the mouse-contingent display: on more than 5% of choice trials, the cursor never entered any dimension's reveal aperture, indicating the participant was not using the display to sample information. A further 20 were excluded for anomalously fast learning-phase responding: more than 20% of a participant's learning-trial response times fell below the 10th percentile of the pooled learning-trial RT distribution (computed after the mousequality exclusions above; threshold = 627ms), suggesting responses made without engaging with the stimuli. The final N was thus 109 participants (age M = 29.5, SD = 4.9, range 18-37; 56 male, 49 female, 4 gender-nonbinary). Participants were paid $8 an hour for their participation as well as a flat $3 bonus for completing the task. Overall, the experimental session took 40-60 minutes. The experiment was approved by the University of Chicago SSD IRB (IRB number: IRB20-1324).

Dimension Learning Task: Participants learned value-category mappings for three perceptually continuous dimensions. The first of these was a shape space with morphs drawn from a validated circular shape space (Li et al. 2020). Participants also learned value-category mappings for hues sampled from a circle in CIELAB space centered at (𝐿 * = 50, 𝑎 * = 20, 𝑏 * = 20) with a radius (chroma) of 60, with hue angle varying 1-231° to generate the category space. The final dimension was a line angle, where participants saw the orientation of an indicator line within a circle. The line's orientation swept a 230° arc centered on vertical, from 8 o'clock through 12 o'clock to 4 o'clock. Each dimension was thus parameterized by an angle restricted to 1-231° of its underlying circular space, and this range was divided into five value categories spanning 30° of arc each (31 integer angles per category), separated by 20° gaps, so that category centers are 50° apart (Figure 1 B). Each category was assigned a point value (e.g., for a given dimension: Category1 = 0 points, Category2 = 25 points, Category3 = 50 points, Category4 = 75, Category5 = 100 points). The mapping direction (ascending vs. descending along the perceptual continuum) was counterbalanced across participants.

On each learning trial, participants saw two stimuli from different value categories on the same dimension and were prompted to select the higher-or lower-value option. The prompt alternated every 10 trials so as not to associate one end of the scale with more selections. On every trial, a feedback screen indicated both correctness as well as the values of each option. Participants completed learning blocks for each dimension sequentially (order randomized). Each block was 40 trials in length such that participants performed 4 choices for each pairwise comparison between the categories.

Multi-Dimensional Decision Task: After learning, participants completed a multi-dimensional decision task in which they were asked to choose between two multi-attribute options presented as columns. Participants completed 120 trials, with a self-paced break (up to one minute) at the midpoint. On each of these trials, they were shown a 250 ms preview of two multi-attribute options, each containing one stimulus from each dimension (row positions randomized but matched across columns). The stimuli were then occluded (gray-scaled, lowered resolution). They could then reveal individual dimensions by hovering the mouse cursor over each location (100 pixel aperture radius). This created a serial processing bottleneck that allowed us to record information sampling trajectories. After sampling, participants then selected the option they believed carried the higher aggregate value by clicking a button on the bottom of the screen corresponding to either the left or right option. Responding was self-paced up to a 2-minute limit per trial. No feedback was provided on any trial, though participants were informed that their choices accrued points toward their bonus. Trials were separated by a centrally placed fixation cross that participants clicked to begin the next trial; this returned the cursor to the center of the screen, equidistant from the two options, before each preview. Before the main task, participants completed six practice trials with the mouse-contingent display in which they searched an occluded display for a single target stimulus ("Which side has the purple?"), familiarizing them with the aperture and the response buttons.

In this task, our main trials of interest are those where the magnitude calculation and the tally calculation point to different options. We label these trials incongruent trials. These arise when one dimension exhibits a large value difference that outweighs two smaller opposing differences on the remaining dimensions. That single dimension is called the critical dimension. For example, if Option A scores [100, 25, 25] and Option B scores [25, 50, 50] across three dimensions, then: Magnitude winner = Option A (summed EV: 150 vs. 125); Tally winner = Option B (wins on 2 of 3 dimensions; Figure 2 B).

As there are 3 dimensions, we generated an equivalent number of incongruent trials where the critical dimension was shape, color, and line orientation (20 trials each). Overall, there were 60 objectively incongruent trials per participant. There was also an equivalent number of non-incongruent trials (where both calculations point the participant towards the same option). The set of trials were constructed to guarantee sufficient incongruent trials (random composites yield too few).

Knowledge measures: For each participant and dimension, we defined dimension knowledge as the mean accuracy across the 40-trial learning block acc 𝑠𝑑 . Overall value knowledge 𝐾 𝑠 is the mean of the three values, z-scored across participants.
