To measure how much distance between categories guided learning, we fit a hierarchical logistic regression of learning-trial accuracy on the category distance of the pair, with a slope per participant and dimension (logits per category step); a participant's ΔValue slope is the mean of their three slopes. To test whether dimension knowledge and dimension weight coupled within participants, we double-centered the weights, removing each participant's overall gain and each dimension's overall learnability. Category distance tracks both perceptual and value distance in this design, so the slope cannot separate the two.

Mouse sampling analyses:

In order to examine sampling dynamics, we first constructed areas of interest that aligned with each of the 3 attributes for both the left and right option (6 total AOIs). Transitions were defined over the sequence of dimensional AOI visits within a trial. At each mouse sample we determined which of the six dimensional AOIs (one per attribute per side, 100-pixelradius circles centered on each stimulus location) contained the cursor, if any; samples falling outside all six AOIs were left unassigned. Consecutive samples occupying the same AOI were collapsed into a single visit, so that dwelling within one region did not itself generate additional transitions; a visit was retained only if its cumulative dwell reached 100 ms, which excludes brief transits through an AOI that lay on the cursor's path between two other regions (e.g., passing through the middle aperture of one option while moving between its top and bottom attributes) rather than reflecting a genuine attentional stop. A transition was then defined as any adjacent pair of visits in this filtered, collapsed sequence (e.g., left-shape → right-shape → right-color yields two transitions). Each transition was classified along two dimensions: whether it crossed sides (between-option, left↔right, vs. within-option, same side), and, among side-crossing transitions, whether the attribute also matched. Only a side-crossing transition that landed on the same attribute (e.g., left-shape → right-shape) was classified as attribute-wise; a side-crossing transition that also changed attribute (e.g., left-shape → right-color, a diagonal move) reflects neither an option-wise nor an attribute-wise comparison and was tallied separately. With 3 attributes and 2 options, each AOI has 5 possible destinations (2 within-option, 1 attribute-wise, 2 diagonal), so a searcher choosing among them at random produces within-option and attribute-wise moves at a 2:1 ratio rather than 1:1, and pooling them directly is therefore not centered on chance. We used the Payne index (Payne 1976), adapted with the chance correction of Böckenholt and Hynan (1994), as our primary measure of scanning behavior. Each total count was divided by its number of possible transitions (2 for within-option, 1 for attribute-wise) before differencing, computed per trial as (within-option/2 -attribute-wise/1) / (within-option/2 + attribute-wise/1). Positive values thus indicate predominantly option-wise (alternative-based) search, e.g. scanning the attributes within one option before moving to the other; negative values indicate predominantly attributewise search, e.g. scanning back and forth between the two options within an attribute; a value of 0 corresponds to chance-level search rather than to an equal raw count of within-and betweenoption moves. Along with the Payne index, we calculated sampling depth as the number of (dwellfiltered) AOI visits in a trial's collapsed visit sequence (revisits to a previously viewed AOI count as new visits), averaged across trials within participant; it indexes how much information a participant gathered per trial, independent of the between/within composition of their transitions. We also computed, per participant and attribute, that attribute's share of aperture visits, of first reveals, and of attribute-wise comparisons. Following prior literature, we call dimension-wise transitions attribute-wise in these analyses. To relate search to the choice model, we fit one regression with the Payne index and sampling depth as joint outcomes and magnitude reliance, concentration, and gain entered together as predictors.

Value regressors: To generate the value regressors we first converted the 5 categories for each stimulus into a level index. We do not use the point values directly as we estimate them within the choice model. This means that for participant 𝑠 on trial 𝑡 and dimension 𝑑 we record the taught level of each option, 𝑘 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 , 𝑘 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 ∈ {0, … , 4}, along with the within-category position of each stimulus, 𝜑 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 , 𝜑 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 ∈ [-0.5, +0.5]. This within-category position is the stimulus's location inside its own 30°-wide category band, expressed as a signed fraction of the category with 𝜑 = 0 at the midpoint, signed so that 𝜑 > 0 always means toward the higher-value end of that dimension. Because the taught point value is constant within a category by construction, 𝜑 carries no taught value at all. Thus, it only adds weight if participants interpolate between the taught levels.

Table 1: Model parameters Symbol Definition Measured, not fitted acc 𝑠𝑑 dimension knowledge: how well participant 𝑠 learned dimension 𝑑 𝐾 𝑠 value knowledge: a participant's mean of the three, standardized 𝑘 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 , 𝑘 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 which of the five taught levels each option's stimulus belonged to 𝜑 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 , 𝜑 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 where inside its own 30° band the stimulus sat, signed toward the higher-value end Estimated inside the choice model 𝑢 𝑠𝑑 (𝑐) the value map: what each taught level is actually worth, on a 0-1 scale with the ends pinned 𝛾 𝑑 within-band slope: how much value a full traverse of one band buys; 0 means five labels and nothing between them 𝑤 𝑠𝑑 weight: how much dimension 𝑑 counts for participant 𝑠 𝜆 𝑠 magnitude reliance: how much of the comparison uses the size of each difference rather than only its direction (1 = weighted sum,0 = weighted vote) 𝑎 0 , 𝜎 𝑎 side bias: a participant's standing left/right preference Derived from the fitted weights ‖𝑊 𝑠 ‖ gain: mean ‖𝑤 𝑠𝑑 ‖; how strongly value drove the choice at all conc 𝑠 concentration: Gini of ‖𝑤 𝑠𝑑 ‖; whether weight is spread across dimensions or piled on one (0 = equal, 2/3 = one cue only) Mouse tracking Payne index option-wise vs attribute-wise search; positive = scanning within an option, negative = comparing one attribute across options sampling depth how many aperture visits a trial took

Choice model: We use the above value regressors to fit a choice model with three components.

The first is a value map (𝑢 𝑠𝑑 (𝑐)) that maps taught levels to a subjective utility estimate. The second is a mixture (𝜆 𝑠 ) of the two readings of each dimension's difference on that map (its size or only its direction). The third is a set of dimension weights (𝑤 𝑠𝑑 ) that sum the readings into the log-odds of choosing the option on the right.

We first estimate the value map as participants will have learned the value representations of each dimension to varying degrees. For each participant and for each dimension, we fit a free monotone value map 𝑢 𝑠𝑑 over the five taught levels:

We pin the low end of the value map to 0 and fit 3 value category variables for each participant and dimension that define the increments between the value levels (𝜓 𝑠𝑑 ). We then use a softmax over the fitted value category variables to make the individual participant value maps well-behaved.

The taught category levels are then combined with the within-category positions to estimate the overall value of a given option and thus the difference is:

When both stimuli fall in the same category 𝑘 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 = 𝑘 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 , the value difference is objectively 0 but we estimate 𝛾 𝑑 (𝜑 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 -𝜑 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 ), where 𝛾 𝑑 is a per dimension weight on how within-category location enters overall utility. Note that 𝛾 𝑑 is shared across participants because we capture participant level variation in the shape of the value map using the per-participant increments (𝜓 𝑠𝑑 ). Further, setting 𝜓 = 0 makes all four increments equal and 𝛾 𝑑 = 0 removes the within-band slope, which together reproduce a value map linear in the taught points. Thus if the values of those parameters differ meaningfully from 0 it indicates that participants learn a warped and graded value map. Fitted, the maps were graded rather than five flat labels: value varied with position inside a taught category at M = 0.81, 95% HDI [0.65, 0.97] of a fully graded map (Supplement S6).

There are two primary ways to read a value map, either by examining the distance or collapsing this to an ordinal read over the sign. We provide these two reads as two separate channels to the model to guide choice. The magnitude channel uses distance between options on the fitted value map (Δ𝑢 𝑑𝑡 ), whereas the ordinal channel keeps only which option is ahead, sign(𝑘 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 -𝑘 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 ), on the raw ±1 scale. Because the fitted value map is strictly monotone in taught level, the ordinal channel is identical whether computed on taught points or on fitted values. The two channels are two readings of the same difference vector and are correlated across trials (0.63), though the trial set was constructed so that 50% of trials place them in conflict (e.g. incongruent trials).

We next combine the above difference in option values using the two channels. We use a mixture parameter (𝜆 𝑠 ) that we term magnitude reliance, which sets how the participant combines the two value channels. At 𝜆 𝑠 = 1, only the magnitude channel is used such that a large advantage on one attribute can outweigh losses on the others. Conversely at 𝜆 𝑠 = 0, only the ordinal channel guides choice such that the deciding factor is winning on 2/3 attributes. Intermediate values mix the two channels.

(1 -𝜆 𝑠 ) sign(𝑘 𝑅 𝑑𝑡 -𝑘 𝐿 𝑑𝑡 ) + 𝜆 𝑠 Δ𝑢 𝑑𝑡 A per-dimension weight then combines the values read from the map into an overall choice value for each option. These options are then compared and used to predict the probability of choosing the right-side option.

where 𝑎 0 + 𝜎 𝑎 𝑧 𝑎 𝑠 is a participant-specific side bias. Weights are hierarchical, 𝑤 𝑠𝑑 = 𝑤 𝜇𝑑 + 𝜎 𝑤 𝑧 𝑤 𝑠𝑑 (dimension-specific group mean, one shared between-participant spread), and 𝜆 𝑠 = logit -1 (𝑐 0 + 𝜎 𝜆 𝑧 𝜆 𝑠 ).

For further analyses, we derive two summaries of the fitted weight vector. The first of these is gain:

This measure indexes how strongly a participant used their value map to guide choice. The second summary we derive is concentration (Gini coefficient of absolute weights):

This measure begins at 0 when all three weights are equal to one another and has a maximum value of 2/3 when all weight sits on one dimension.

Inference: For the inferential statistics we report a series of Bayesian hierarchical regressions.

As both the choice model and the regressions are hierarchical Bayesian models, in the remainder of the text we use the term model for the choice model variants and inferential statistics use the term regression. All variants of the choice model used 4 chains with 1500 warmup and 1500 draws (target-accept 0.90-0.95); cross-validation refits were reduced in scale (600-1500 warmup and draws). Throughout the paper each estimate, both for the choice model parameters as well as regression results, is reported as its posterior mean (𝑀) followed by the 95% HDI in brackets.

We use weakly-informative priors on our standardized predictors. Participant-level predictors of a latent quantity (gain, 𝜆 𝑠 ) entered that quantity's prior inside the choice model. We estimated these predictors at the same time as the latent parameter as correlating them with posterior estimates instead would bias the result toward zero. R ≤ 1.008 on all fits of record, divergences 0; the full fits table is in Supplement S1.

Model comparison and validation:

Candidate models were compared by five-fold trial-wise cross-validation, the choice model in the main text was validated by generate-and-refit identifiability, parameter recovery, and forward posterior predictive checks (Supplement S2-S5). Magnitude reliance 𝜆 𝑠 had high split-half reliability (0.89, Spearman-Brown corrected, with each participant's trials split into halves), and the 95% HDIs of recovered parameters covered the generating values 91% of the time (Supplement S4).

Pointer-device control: At debrief participants reported whether they had used a mouse or a trackpad (64 mouse, 45 trackpad). Because pointer hardware changes the motor cost of moving the cursor between screen locations, and the display is mouse-contingent, we refit every analysis reported below with device included as a covariate (sum-coded, trackpadmouse), entering the choice model as a shift on both the whole weight vector and magnitude reliance. Device was not credible on either parameter (magnitude reliance M = -0.49, 95% HDI [-1.24, +0.27]; weights M = -0.47, 95% HDI [-0.99, +0.04]) and no reported result moves: the knowledge couplings shift by < 0.01 (gain-knowledge +0.47, magnitude reliance-knowledge 0.00). The device-adjusted refits of every coefficient are in Supplement S8.

Software:

The experiment was built using jsPsych 7.3 (Leeuw 2015). The choice models were fit using the NumPyro package (v0.20.0) in python; the descriptive hierarchical regressions (psychometric and cross-stream analyses) were fit with bambi/PyMC using the nutpie sampler. Experimental and analytic code are available at the following github: https://github.com/BakkourLabRepo/cactus-paper

Deviations from pre-registration: We pre-registered the experiment before collecting the sample reported in this manuscript. We report the results of the 4 pre-registered frequentist tests, and every deviation from the pre-registration, in Supplement S9.

Results

We examined how participants constructed the value of novel multi-dimensional options when the dimensions were learned to uneven degrees. We find that participants vary in how well they learned the dimensions both within and across participants. That variation enters downstream value construction and choice through two different routes; how the dimensions are weighted for the composite, and how individuals read their value maps for direction or distance. Each of these has its own predecessor in the dimensional learning task. Finally, we find that these two routes also leave different fingerprints in the search process.

Participants learn different dimensions to varying degrees of specificity

First we examined learning and found that participants learned the three dimensions unevenly. Across participants, shape was modally learned best and line modally worst, with every pairwise difference credible (Supplement S7). The unevenness between dimensions was also present within participants, on average, a participant's best-learned dimension beat their worst by 0.23 in accuracy (best / middle / worst = 0.88 / 0.79 / 0.66; Figure 1 C).

Accuracy also rose with the value distance between the two categories being compared (group slope M = +0.18, 95% HDI [+0.12, +0.23]), and participants differed widely in how steeply it rose (SD of slopes M = +0.21, 95% HDI [+0.13, +0.30]). This ΔValue slope is a separate measure from 𝐾 𝑠 (propagated M = +0.13, 95% HDI [-0.03, +0.29]), though it is noisy for any one participant (split-half r = 0.07; Supplement S7).

Figure 1: Dimension learning task. A.

Each learning trial presented two stimuli from different value categories of one dimension. Participants were given 3000ms to judge which item was higher or lower according to a prompt. They were then shown a green box around their selection if they made the correct choice and a red box if they made an incorrect choice. We showed the points values above both the selected and non-selected options to speed learning. B. The stimulus is drawn from an angle in a circular perceptual space, cut into five value categories worth 0-100 points. For each dimension we constrained the angles to only span from 1-231° with 20° gaps between value categories to aid in learning.

Choices are driven by overall knowledge

As participants learned the individual value maps with varying sensitivity to varying accuracy, we next sought to examine how these varied representations are combined in the service of the construction of a single value. In the multi-dimensional decision task, participants viewed pairs of composite items, each composed of a novel combination of color, a shape, and a line angle, and selected the option they believed was more valuable (Figure 2 A). As each of the dimensions carried a distinct value participants could use one of two methods to determine which choice was better. They could sum the values for each dimension within each option before comparing which option has the higher sum (a magnitude difference). They might also discard the explicit magnitude and simply compare dimensions on strength across options tallying up which option wins on more dimensions. On congruent trials both the magnitude and tally point in the same direction but we explicitly introduce incongruent configurations on 50% of trials such that magnitude and tallybased comparisons will select different winners (Figure 2 B). We first examined whether participants differed in accuracy in the multi-dimensional decision task as a function of their overall value knowledge from the dimension learning task. We fit a hierarchical logistic regression to assess the effect of value difference, value knowledge, as well as their interaction. We found that difference in value between left and right options guided choice (|ΔValue| M = +1.82, 95% HDI [+1.62, +2.01]), and further that this slope steepened with overall value knowledge (|ΔValue| × 𝐾 M = +0.52, 95% HDI [+0.34, +0.73] ; Figure 3 A). Participants with better dimensional learning use their value maps to drive choice than those with lower dimensional learning accuracy.

We next examined how accuracy differed for congruent and incongruent trials. On congruent trials accuracy was generally high (mean = 0.88 , SD = 0.12). Congruent trials allow both strategies to guide individuals to the correct answer and thus can be fairly straightforward. On incongruent trials, where the magnitude calculation pointed towards the correct answer and the dimensiontally disagreed, overall accuracy fell to near chance (0.53) and its between-participant spread increased (0.12 → 0.20). This indicated that participants varied widely in how much they relied on the magnitude of the value differences rather than only on their direction. Indeed, this can be seen in the spread across the average correctness on incongruent trials across participants (Figure 3 B).

Value is constructed from the map in two different ways

Participant variation in magnitude versus direction reliance can come from two sources. Given that participants learn the value maps, they might compose them with different weights akin to reliability weighting, but based on knowledge instead of environmental reliability. They may also differ in how they read their maps to guide value construction and choice, relying on either magnitude of the value differences or simple ordinal directions.

We examined how participants may weight the composite option by fitting one weight per dimension per participant (weight vector: 𝑊 𝑠 ; Figure 4 A). After estimating a weight per dimension for every participant, we find that dimensions did not count equally (color M = +4.02, 95% HDI [+3.60, +4.43], shape M = +3.83, 95% HDI [+3.42, +4.22], line M = +3.11, 95% HDI [+2.71, +3.52]). Furthermore, participants differed widely in these weights, such that participants were not all weighting the same dimension highly and the other two lower (M = +1.98, 95% HDI [+1.76, +2.18]). We further summarize each participant's weight vector by its gain |𝑊 𝑠 |, the mean absolute weight, and its concentration, which captures how uneven the weights are across dimensions (Gini coefficient).

Given a specific set of weights to construct a composite there's also a way to read that composite.

For instance participants can use some mixture of magnitude or directional information from the map to guiding their choice. If participants were using a pure directional strategy, it should not matter whether the left side wins by +25 or by +100 (Figure 4 C purple line). If instead participants were using each value difference, one would expect an equivalent change in choice for each of the Δvalue increments (Figure 4 C orange line). We investigated how participants used magnitude or direction information in choice by fitting a mixing parameter (𝜆). This models participants as integrating between the sign information used in a tally and the value differences from their map (at 𝜆 = 0 they are only using sign, at 𝜆 = 1 they are only using the map; see Methods: Choice Model).

We find that participants perform a mixture of these two reads, with a dimension won by 25 points carrying 0.40 [0.37, 0.44] of the weight of one won by 100. When we use only the participant's fitted value map on size, a 25-point win should count for 0.25 [0.24, 0.26]. A direction-only read gives a 25-point and a 50-point win the same weight, which no value map read by size can do. This parameter spanned nearly the full range available to it across participants (mean 0.76, min 0.14-max 0.99). It was also reliable and recoverable (Methods).

As we examined the dimensional learning task using both general accuracy as well as distance sensitivity, we sought to understand whether the weights or the magnitude reliance during readout were guided by accuracy or sensitivity. We expected that knowledge of the specific dimensions should alter their weighting during choice. Meanwhile, the sensitivity to distance at learning should cause participants to be more sensitive to distance in their readout. We tested all four couplings inside the choice model, entering each learning measure as a predictor of a participant's gain (a multiplicative scale on their weights) and of 𝜆 𝑠 , first alone and then both together (Methods:

Inference).

Overall value knowledge 𝐾 𝑠 predicted gain across participants, such that participants with better dimension learning task accuracy used the values they learned more strongly in novel choices (M = +0.13, 95% HDI [+0.07, +0.20]; with the ΔValue slope in the same model M = +0.17, 95% HDI [+0.10, +0.24]; Figure 4 B). Knowledge and weight also coupled within participants, where the better-learned dimension carried more weight (double-centered and propagated M = +0.32, 95% HDI [+0.27, +0.36], across 327 participant × dimension cells; Supplement S7, Figure S2A). Unexpectedly, a steeper ΔValue slope predicted lower gain (M = -0.09, 95% HDI [-0.16, -0.02]) opposite in sign to 𝐾 𝑠 . Thus, knowing how well the participant did at the dimension learning task, and at which dimensions specifically, guides how they construct value for novel composites during choice.

We found 𝜆 𝑠 was not credibly related to 𝐾 𝑠 (M = +0.14, 95% HDI [-0.29, +0.55]; with the ΔValue slope in the same model M = +0.32, 95% HDI [-0.10, +0.73]), although the estimate rose once the slope was controlled, as the two learning measures partly mask each other. This could be a function of the demands of the dimension learning task, which only ever asked an ordinal question.

Thus, overall value knowledge may only index order learning. When we enter the ΔValue slope (Figure 1 C) as a predictor of 𝜆 𝑠 inside the choice model, we find it is credibly negative (M = -0.44, 95% HDI [-0.84, -0.04]).

Learners whose accuracy depended more on separation, meanwhile, read the map more by direction, becoming more tally-like (Figure 4 D; shallow 0.58, middle 0.58, steep 0.71). The same split by 𝐾 𝑠 did not order the profile (high minus low tertile M = -0.06, 95% HDI [-0.16, +0.01]), whereas the split by slope did (steep minus shallow M = +0.13, 95% HDI [+0.05, +0.21]), and more so once 𝐾 𝑠 was regressed out of the slope (M = +0.17, 95% HDI [+0.08, +0.27]; Supplement S7, Figure S2B).

Learning thus reached value construction by two overlapping routes. The first of which depended on how well a dimension was learned set its weight. Meanwhile, how strongly learning depended on separation lowered both the weights and the reliance on magnitude. The weights and magnitude reliance were separable but not independent. Concentration was orthogonal to magnitude reliance (M = +0.01, 95% HDI [-0.09, +0.12]) whereas gain was credibly related to it (M = +0.25, 95% HDI [+0.13, +0.36]; the full strategy plane is in Supplement S10).

Value dimension weighting and map readout leave separate markers in search

If the two constructs from the model (i.e., weights and magnitude reliance) are separate, they should surface in different components of how participants searched for information. We found that participants had a wide variance in how they sampled the multi-dimensional decision task stimulus display while comparing the two options (mean -0.20, SD 0.30, range -0.80 to +0.74; 21% above 0; Figure 5 A for examples). We regressed the Payne index (Payne 1976) and sampling depth jointly on magnitude reliance, concentration, and gain (Methods).

Magnitude reliance predicted the Payne index with both weight constructs held fixed (M = +0.13, 95% HDI [+0.07, +0.18]; Figure 5 B). Neither weight construct was credible in the same regression (gain M = -0.03, 95% HDI [-0.10, +0.03], concentration M = +0.059, 95% HDI [-0.003, +0.121]), and the paired contrast favored magnitude reliance over gain (M = +0.09, 95% HDI [+0.03, +0.15]; case-bootstrap mean and 95% CI. C. How much winning one dimension by 25 to 100 points contributes to choice, relative to a 100-point win (pooled logistic regression). Black, observed, with 95% participantbootstrap intervals. Orange (dashed), choices simulated with magnitude reliance fixed at 1, so that each participant reads their own fitted value map by size alone; purple (dashed), magnitude reliance fixed at 0, so that each participant counts only which option is ahead on each dimension; dark gray, the full model; shading, 95% interval across 200 posterior draws.

D. The same profile, split into tertiles of the ΔValue slope: how steeply a participant's single-dimension learning accuracy rose with the value difference between the two categories compared (light gray, shallow; black, steep).

Markers and bars are the pooled estimate and 95% participant-bootstrap interval; the dotted line marks a pure tally. Inset: each tertile's fitted learning curve (accuracy against the ΔValue of the pair, averaged over its participants' model-implied curves). The split comes from the dimension learning task, so the choice model is not involved. In C and D the y-axis starts at 0.25 (break mark), so a contribution proportional to the points won is a straight line through the lower-left corner.

Figure 5 C; against concentration M = +0.07, 95% HDI [-0.02, +0.16]). Participants that rely on magnitude information are therefore performing search in a way commensurate with within-option summation. Magnitude reliance did not credibly predict sampling depth (M = +0.08, 95% HDI [-0.10, +0.26]), but the crossing between them was positive and not credible (M = +0.21, 95% HDI [-0.02, +0.45]; Figure 5 E).

Sampling depth, meanwhile, was better predicted by concentration (M = -0.31, 95% HDI [-0.54, -0.10]; Figure 5 D). Gain and concentration are two moments of the same three-vector and are correlated (-0.57), partly mechanically, and the contrast between them does not separate them either (M = +0.10, 95% HDI [-0.28, +0.46]). We therefore claim that depth tracks the weight vector, and that magnitude reliance is more attuned to the pattern of sampling rather than the depth. Allocation followed the weights dimension by dimension. A dimension's weight predicted its share of aperture visits (M = +0.01, 95% HDI [+0.01, +0.01]), its share of first reveals (M = +0.02, 95% HDI [+0.01, +0.03]), and its share of dimension-wise comparisons (M = +0.01, 95% HDI [+0.01, +0.02]). We added per-dimension learning accuracy to the same regression. It added nothing given weight (M = 0.00, 95% HDI [-0.03, +0.03]) and left the weight coefficient unmoved (M = +0.01, 95% HDI [+0.01, +0.01]). Thus the impact of knowledge on search is mediated by the fitted weights. Search therefore separates the two constructs more cleanly than learning does.

Discussion

Often when we make new decisions, we base them on knowledge learned across experience and organized in a cognitive map (Bellmund et al. 2018). There has been a great deal of research on how individuals make multi-dimensional decisions, finding a variety of strategies that appear rationally matched to the environment (Binz et al. 2022; Krueger et al. 2024). As these studies supply attribute values directly, they leave open how people read a value map they have learned themselves. Here, we taught participants value maps for each dimension to see how value is constructed. We find that what is often called a single "strategy" in multi-dimensional choice bundles together two components of value construction: 1) how much each dimension counts (weight) and 2) how the product of dimension-by-dimension learning (i.e., the map) is read at choice time (by direction [which option is better] or by magnitude [how much better is one option]). Further, we find that different aspects of the learning process affect these two components, and that each of these two streams leaves separate imprints in how participants search during value construction.

Because incongruent trials force the two ways of reading the map to disagree, and the choice model estimates both components from the same choices, the two could be separated here. By linking performance in a separate learning phase to choices among never-seen composites, we could further connect individual differences in value construction to what was actually learned, which is not possible when values are estimated from ratings. Previous work in the adaptive toolbox literature treats weighting and reading, among others, as selection from a repertoire, indexed to environment structure (Gigerenzer and Goldstein 1996; Payne 1976; Rieskamp and Otto 2006), and discrete strategies can emerge from a resource rational process that maps onto both envi-map by size predicts 25% (Figure 4 C), and no reshaping or warping of that map can produce the difference. Instead, participants stably differ in how they perform their comparisons over a given representation. The literature on cognitive maps establishes that participants consult geometric representations during value-based choice (Park, Miller, and Boorman 2021; J.-Y. Son, Bhandari, and FeldmanHall 2021) but rarely asks how that geometry is explicitly traversed during the construction of value for a novel option.

Here, better-learned dimensions carried more weight. Differential weighting can occur due to uncertainty about the stimulus. In literature on cue combination, participants are shown to weight dimensions according to reliability, such that dimensions that are more reliable are given higher weight in the composite stimulus (Ernst and Banks 2002; Bankieris, Bejjanki, and Aslin 2017).

Another alternative is that the weights can differ due to differences in how predictive they are for reward, with participants learning to allocate attention to those dimensions that are more relevant (Niv et al. 2015; Leong et al. 2017; Maher et al., n.d.). In our study, each dimension is equally rewarded and equally reliable but differs in how participants learn and represent them. Thus our weighting could stem from attentional differences or from differences in internal reliability of learned value maps.

The way in which participants allocate sampling gives further evidence for an attentional mechanism. We found that the readout and weights are dissociated in how individuals sample information during the task. Where participants allocate their aperture tracks their weights, similarly to how gaze tracks attribute weights in attentional models of multi-attribute choice (Yang and Krajbich 2023), and which would be predicted by the weighting following attention. The amount of sampling also tracks the weight vector, consistent with search directed by attribute importance (Gluth, Deakin, and Rieskamp 2026). The pattern with which they moved between the dimensional cues, however, tracked the readout parameter. Current attentional decision models link attention to how much each attribute counts (Krajbich, Armel, and Rangel 2010; Yang and Krajbich 2023; Gluth, Deakin, and Rieskamp 2026), and our results suggest they should also distinguish how the evidence gathered is read. Because sampling and readout were measured together, this design cannot tell whether attribute-wise sampling shapes the readout or the readout shapes sampling, and the two may well inform each other (Braunlich and Love 2022).

In our work, the features that make the readout estimable also limit our ability to understand how individuals might calibrate their readout in response to changing task demands. More specifically, the lack of feedback during the multi-dimensional decision task means that individuals had no signal to calibrate their readout to a more optimal strategy. Future work can provide feedback during this task to examine whether participants adjust strategies to ongoing demands. We further designed the task to incorporate a 50% rate of incongruent trials to better estimate the readout parameter. If composites were instead drawn randomly, the expected rate of incongruent trials would be much lower. Finally, while our mouse contingent aperture design allows us to examine information sampling without the use of an eyetracker it could induce a cost to serial sampling that is less present in free-viewing content.

Taken together, our findings suggest that the question of what strategy do people use to construct value from a learned map is better split into two: 1. how much does each dimension of the map count? and 2. how is the map read? Both can be traced to what people learned but leave dissociable marks in choice and information search. The distinction matters because people routinely choose among new combinations of attributes they only previously learned piecemeal, such as products, job offers, or partners. Our results imply that two people can make different decisions because they learned the dimensions relevant to choice to different degrees (i.e., they formed different maps) or because they read the same map differently, despite identical learning. The results argue for treating learned representations and the operations performed on them as separate components within computational modeling frameworks. The results also have implications for policies that set decision environments and choice aids. Finally, this work highlights that learned value maps are not just stored but also composed and read out at the time of choice. Differences in the latter steps help explain why two people with the same knowledge may nonetheless choose a different cocktail.

Instead of assigning discrete heuristics to individuals, the mixture parameter placed nearly everyone between a purely direction-driven strategy (akin to a weighted tally (Russo and Dosher 1983)) and a purely magnitude-driven one (akin to WADD (Payne, Bettman, and Johnson 2008)). Classical strategies are thus the corners of a continuous plane that participants sit within (only 7 of 109 effectively one-cue; Supplement S10). Figure 1: Dimension learning task. A.Each learning trial presented two stimuli from different value categories of one dimension. Participants were given 3000ms to judge which item was higher or lower according to a prompt. They were then shown a green box around their selection if they made the correct choice and a red box if they made an incorrect choice. We showed the points values above both the selected and non-selected options to speed learning. B. The stimulus is drawn from an angle in a circular perceptual space, cut into five value categories worth 0-100 points. For each dimension we constrained the angles to only span from 1-231° with 20° gaps between value categories to aid in learning. C. (Left) Learning accuracy over the 40 trials of a block, shape is quickly learned to a high level whereas color and line lag behind. The dashed lines at trials 11, 21 and 31 mark the prompt reversals, which account for the dips in performance. Bands are 95% CI. (Right) Accuracy against the |ΔValue| of the pair, per dimension: thin lines, each participant's fitted curve from the hierarchical learning model; black line, pooled logistic fit; markers, participant means ± 95% CI.

Figure 2: Multi-dimensional decision task. A.Example trials: two composite options, each a novel combination of a color, a shape, and a line angle (one dimension per row, row order randomized per trial); participants chose the option they judged more valuable. B. The two trial types. On incongruent trials the summed magnitude (Σ favoring option A) and the attribute-win tally (option B wins 2/3) point to different options; on congruent trials they agree. Values shown are for illustration, during the task participants never saw explicit values and instead relied on their learned value maps. C. The reveal sequence of a single trial: all six attributes appeared briefly (250 ms), then the display was hidden and dimensions were uncovered one at a time through a mouse-contingent aperture as the cursor moved.
